Economics | 3

### Page 4

Monthly Economics
Sector Analysis
Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE)
• We've lifted our Q3 expectation for real PCE on incoming data and a slightly firmer consumer
backdrop in the wake of recent revisions.
Consumer spending looks firmer in the third quarter, and we now expect real PCE to rise at a 3.4%
annualized clip. This upward revision reflects recent data, with August consumption coming in firm
with real spending rising by the fastest monthly clip in nearly two years, but also reflects a slightly

firmer consumer backdrop in the wake of the BEA's annual revisions released late September. Although
revisions did not materially alter spending, they lifted the level of household income in recent years,
which also boosted the personal saving rate. The trajectory of real income growth remains weak, but
the consumer still looks to be in a slightly stronger financial position than previously believed, which
helps explain recent resilience in spending and suggests consumers may have a bit more runway ahead.

Investment: Equipment, Intellectual Property Products and Inventories
• The AI build out will lead to another surge in business fixed investment spending in Q3, though the
effect on headline growth will be partly offset by stronger imports.
Business fixed investment spending continues to be supported primarily by the high-tech buildout.
We forecast real equipment investment to rise at around a 16% annualized clip in Q3, but part of
this boost to headline GDP growth will be offset by stronger import growth that is facilitating the

build out. Net imports of computers are tracking to surge in the third quarter, which will provide a
sizable lift to information processing equipment investment, or around half of overall equipment.
And while non-defense capital goods shipments (including aircraft) slipped in August, the near-term
growth trajectory remains supportive of a pickup in equipment. The rebuild in inventories we have
been anticipating is also tracking to be a bit stronger as businesses replenish drawn down product.

Investment: Residential
• The abrupt rise in mortgage rates is likely to dampen residential investment over the next few
quarters. Residential spending should find support next year as longer-term interest rates
moderate somewhat.
We are unlikely to see another positive residential investment reading in Q3. Rising mortgage rates

have sent a chill over builder outlooks, pushing sales expectations to their weakest level in more than
three years. While builders have responded with deeper price cuts, affordability pressures continue
to weigh on single-family sales and construction outlays. Apartment construction is simultaneously
soft amid rising financing costs and modest rent growth, and home improvement spending remains
constrained by high materials costs and interest rates. While residential investment is likely to remain

4 | Economics

### Page 5

Economic Outlook: October 2026 Economics
a drag this year, we expect a slight moderation in longer-term interest rates in 2027, which would
provide a more supportive backdrop.
Investment: Nonresidential Structures
• We have not made material changes to our nonresidential structures forecast. Despite elevated
interest rates, data center and related spending should continue to support structures investment
through our forecast horizon.
Nonresidential structures spending has accelerated sharply, overwhelmingly driven by data centers

and electric power facilities. As data center construction expands geographically and demand rises
for semiconductors, high-tech capex has also started to generate positive spillovers for warehousing
and manufacturing construction. This buildout, which appears relatively insensitive to interest rates,
should be enough to sustain solid growth in structures investment even as spending across most
other categories remains essentially flat. Domestic energy is another green shoot, with elevated prices

supporting ongoing growth in active rotary rigs.

Source: U.S. Department of Commerce and Wells Fargo Economics
Net Exports & Trade
• An expected drag from trade in Q3 got larger in light of a near-record trade deficit in August, net
exports is now expected to lop off three full percentage points from third quarter growth.

Exports increased in August for the first time in four months, but the increase was more than offset by
an even larger increase in imports. The upshot was a widening in the trade deficit to a near-record gap
of more than $105 billion. Going into the report, we had a 1.9 percentage point drag from net exports
penciled in for Q3 GDP; we revised that to a full 3.0 percentage points drag in light of the record gap.
Even a modest narrowing in the final months of the year would likely mean a lift to headline GDP in

Q4, but the real story here is that trade will remain a headwind throughout the forecast period owing
largely to surging imports of tech-related products amid the AI boom.
Labor Market
• The jobs market remains stable. We continue to expect the unemployment rate to end the year at
4.2% and hold near that level in 2027.
We modestly revised down our near-term payroll growth forecast following the September jobs
report, but otherwise we have left our labor market outlook unchanged. Measures of labor demand

growth continue to point to a stable labor market. Job openings have inched higher on a year-ago
basis but remain broadly steady while initial jobless claims, WARN notices and Challenger job cut
announcements continue to signal low layoffs. Labor force participation appears to be improving after
its sharp pullback in the first half of the year. Looking ahead, we expect payroll growth to average
around 60K per month in 2027, roughly in line with our estimate of breakeven job growth. That should

Economics | 5

### Page 6

Monthly Economics
keep the unemployment rate near 4.2% in 2027 and leave labor market conditions broadly consistent
with "full employment." Notably, our expectation for labor cost growth of ~3.4% is the reason we
continue to reiterate that labor cost growth is not a contributor to the excess inflation seen in the
data.
Inflation
• We expect the core PCE deflator to rise 3.2% and 2.6% Q4/Q4 in 2026 and 2027, respectively.
Headline inflation remains heavily influenced by developments in the Middle East, with higher oil

prices lifting gasoline and diesel prices. Food inflation has been tame this year but looks poised to
strengthen as higher transportation and commodity prices work their way through supply chains.
Beyond food and energy, price pressures remain concentrated in a handful of categories, most notably
airfares and AI-related tech goods. Core goods inflation is showing signs of stabilizing, as waning tariff-
related price pressures have been offset by resilient demand tied to the AI buildout. We expect some

improvement here in 2027. Still-low shelter inflation and cooler price growth for airfares and health
care should help bring about some moderation in core services inflation next year. We expect the core
PCE deflator to rise 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis in 2026 before slowing to 2.6% in 2027.

Fiscal Policy
• Fiscal year (FY) 2026 ended on September 30, and the federal government tallied a $2.0 trillion
budget deficit (6.5% of GDP). We project the deficit to be unchanged in dollar terms in FY 2027

and a bit smaller as a share of GDP (~6%).
• Another continuing resolution to extend government funding past the December 11 deadline
seems like the most likely outcome at this time for the FY 2027 budget. It is possible Republicans
use the lame duck session of Congress to increase defense spending and raise the debt ceiling.
Looking ahead to the lame duck session of Congress that will follow the midterm elections, Congress
needs to pass a bill that extends government funding past December 11 to avert a government

shutdown. Another continuing resolution that punts the issue into Q1 seems like the most likely
outcome. Perhaps more interesting will be whether Republicans attempt to use budget reconciliation
to pass one more partisan piece of legislation. If they do, we suspect the two things at the top the
agenda will be more funding for national defense and immigration enforcement priorities as well as a
debt ceiling increase. For the latter, raising it in the lame duck would remove some leverage Democrats

could have in 2027 if they recapture one or both chambers. It is still very early, but we estimate the
debt ceiling "X date" by which Congress must act or face default is sometime in late Q3/early Q4 of
next year.
6 | Economics

### Page 7

Economic Outlook: October 2026 Economics
Monetary Policy & Interest Rates
• Our base case forecast is for the FOMC to hike the fed funds rate by 25 bps in December and
then keep the policy rate unchanged in 2027 at 4.00%-4.25%, with risks skewed toward additional
tightening.
As we go to print, the FOMC seems more likely than not to keep the fed funds rate unchanged in
October. But given the rationale under which the Fed started this tightening cycle and the forcefulness

with which Warsh has driven home his displeasure with elevated inflation, it seems entirely plausible
to us that October will be a live meeting. At a minimum, the recent inflation data seem to make a hike
in December a lock. Past December, the outlook will be highly dependent on future inflation data. Our
expectation is that core inflation will cool gradually in 2027, and we still do not see signs of demand-
side inflation in the data. But hiking cycles have an inertial component to them, and the recent inflation

data reset the clock to zero on the Fed seeing several good inflation prints in a row. This creates upside
risk to our base case for the fed funds rate.

International Developments
• Global growth remains resilient, leaving our 2026 and 2027 forecasts unchanged. Our inflation
forecasts are also unchanged, although risks of delayed price pass-through could prolong inflation
pressures into 2027.

• Monetary policy continues to lean toward tightening across advanced economies, while emerging
market central banks are increasingly cautious, either holding rates or easing more gradually as
inflation pressures rebuild.
The balance of risks to global inflation remains tilted to the upside, complicating the monetary policy
outlook even as growth has proven more resilient than expected. Elevated energy prices, particularly
for refined petroleum products, persistent geopolitical uncertainty and El Niño-related food price

risks could prolong inflationary pressures into 2027. While second-round effects still remain relatively
contained, repeated supply shocks are testing central banks’ willingness to look through the temporary
price increases, particularly where inflation expectations remain elevated, even as higher government
bond yields are already contributing to tighter financial conditions.
Across advanced economies, central banks face increasingly difficult trade-offs as persistent inflation

pressures limit the scope for policy accommodation. The Eurozone remains particularly vulnerable
to energy supply disruptions, with elevated natural gas prices and low storage levels adding to
inflation risks. We continue to expect a 25 bps European Central Bank hike in December, followed by a
prolonged hold through 2027. Fiscal concerns are also adding to pressure, particularly in France, where
the selloff in government debt has pushed yields higher. In the U.K., resilient activity and renewed

Economics | 7

### Page 8

Monthly Economics
inflation pressures similarly underpin our expectation for a Bank of England hike in November,
followed by a hold through 2027.
Elsewhere, policy paths are becoming more differentiated. After four hikes this year brought the Cash
Rate to 4.60%, we expect the Reserve Bank of Australia to remain on hold, although renewed inflation
pressures or stronger-than-expected activity could prompt further tightening. We also still expect
the Bank of Canada to hold at 2.25%, with subdued growth weighing against energy-driven inflation

risks, although incoming inflation data over the coming weeks will be critical in determining whether
persistent price pressures warrant tightening monetary policy perhaps as early as the December
meeting. Japan remains an exception to the broader hold outlook: while the yen's recovery provides
some relief from imported inflation, elevated energy prices and import dependence continue to pose
upside risks. We expect the Bank of Japan to hike again by year-end and now expect an additional 25

bps hike in Q3-2027, bringing the terminal rate to 1.75%.
The emerging market story has increasingly shifted from easing toward patience or selective
tightening. In Asia, China's weak domestic demand and persistent property-sector strains continue to
warrant policy support, with AI-related exports providing some offset. We expect a more gradual pace
of Reserve Requirement Ratio cuts alongside targeted monetary and fiscal measures. India faces the

opposite challenge, with elevated food and energy prices and rupee depreciation strengthening the
case for further Reserve Bank of India tightening. In Latin America, peso depreciation and elevated
energy costs pose upside inflation risks for Mexico, although limited exchange-rate pass-through
should allow Banxico to remain on hold while policymakers monitor underlying inflation and inflation
expectations. In Brazil, election-related uncertainty and renewed inflation pressures are likely to delay

further easing, with cuts expected to resume in Q1-2027 once policymakers see more convincing
evidence of disinflation.

### Page 1

The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc.
Global bond yields have risen to multi-decade highs, yet growth and markets have
remained remarkably resilient. But with some pressures beginning to emerge (e.g. in
European sovereign bond markets), can this resilience last, or will more vulnerabilities
be revealed? We ask the IMF’s Tobias Adrian, Moody’s Analytics’ Mark Zandi, and GS
economists. Adrian is relatively sanguine about the economy’s resilience, though
notes downside risks, while GS economists expect only a modest growth drag from

higher rates, but a more consequential one if yields remain near current or higher
levels. Zandi is generally more concerned, describing the US economy as vulnerable
rather than resilient, and sees one-in-three odds of the economy going off the rails.
Meanwhile, GS strategists and analysts are not especially concerned about banks or
markets more broadly, but warn that further increases in yields or rate volatility could
test risk assets. Key to watch: whether the increasingly interconnected and leveraged

AI investment boom can continue to withstand higher rates.
Tobias Adrian, Special Advisor and former Director of Monetary and Capital Markets, IMF
“The global economy has demonstrated considerable resilience in the face of multiple severe shocks, and I would not be
surprised if it continues to shrug off higher yields, provided they do not keep rising sharply in a disorderly fashion. At the same
time, I do worry about downside risks.”
Mark Zandi, Chief Economist, Moody’s Analytics

We provide a brief snapshot on the most important economies for the global markets

### Page 3

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 3 
Top of Mind Issue 152

Recent acceleration in the rise in global bond yields to multi-
decade highs has begun to raise questions about the 
implications for growth and markets. Both have remained 
remarkably resilient so far, with US growth in particular 
holding relatively firm and the S&P 500 and other equity 
markets near new all-time highs. But with pressure 
beginning to emerge—most notably in European sovereign 
bond markets—and yields looking unlikely to retreat soon, 
can that resilience last, or will more vulnerabilities be revealed? 
GS Head of US Rates Strategy William Marshall first lays out 
how we arrived at higher rates, underscoring the striking 
breadth of the rise, which he attributes mainly to 
fundamentals—strong growth, inflation risks, hawkish policy 
shifts, the ongoing investment cycle, and long-run fiscal 
risks—but notes that technical factors likely amplified the 
recent move. And while he ultimately expects yields to fall 
(with YE 10-year US Treasury yields of 4.75%/4.4% in 
2026/2027, respectively) as markets become more confident 
in easing inflation, he thinks elevated long-term yields should 
have some staying power, and sees continued upside risk 
from energy price and fiscal pressures.  
So, just how much of a threat do higher rates pose to the 
macro outlook? For answers, we first turn to Tobias Adrian, 
Special Advisor to the Managing Director and former 
Financial Counsellor and Director of the Monetary and Capital 
Markets Department at the IMF, Mark Zandi, Chief 
Economist of Moody’s Analytics, and GS US economists. 
Adrian sees a global economy that has so far responded to 
the yield shock much as it has to the many other major 
shocks of the past seven years: resiliently. He notes that the 
rise in yields has not significantly tightened financial 
conditions or, more importantly, disrupted market functioning 
or forced damaging deleveraging. He believes that resilience 
could persist as long as the move in yields doesn’t accelerate 
or become more disorderly. But he is concerned about 
vulnerabilities in the AI and technology complex, sovereign 
bond markets, and EMs—not to mention cyber risks—all of 
which leaves him “vigilant but cautiously optimistic.” 
GS US economists also characterize the US economy as 
resilient. Their expectation that the Fed will deliver just one 
more rate hike this year before cutting next year—consistent 
with Marshall’s expectation of lower Treasury yields—leads 
Pierfrancesco Mei to expect only a modest 0.2pp growth 
drag in 2027 from higher rates. But he finds that the drag 
could rise to a more consequential 0.5pp if yields remain near 
current levels (see pg. 9 for GS global economists Joseph 
Briggs’ and Johan Allen’s rules of thumb for the impact of 
higher yields on rate-sensitive areas across major DMs.) 
David Mericle and Mei also worry about the implications for 
the already-concerning US fiscal trajectory, which they 
believe is unlikely to trigger a classic debt crisis but is too risky 
to ignore.     
Zandi, for his part, is generally more concerned. While his 
baseline outlook is relatively sanguine, he describes the US 
economy as vulnerable rather than resilient and views the 
“distribution of possible outcomes as very flat and heavily 
skewed toward darker tail scenarios.” He sees the clearest 
softness in the labor market, with weak hiring and falling real 
wage growth for many workers weighing on consumer 
spending. And he takes little comfort from US growth 
continuing to run around 2%—roughly its potential rate—
noting that AI accounts for roughly one-third of that growth, 
leaving the non-AI economy growing at only around 1-1.5%, 
which he calls “a pretty punk economy.” Zandi also worries 
that the US fiscal situation is “as bad as I have ever seen it” 
and that higher rates are already inflicting damage on real 
estate markets and squeezing existing debtors. As these 
stresses mount, he sees elevated risk of something breaking 
in the financial system. All told, he puts the odds of the 
current vulnerabilities pushing the economy completely off 
the rails at around one-in-three.  
With banks historically at the center of the rate shocks and 
economic crises that Zandi is worried about, we then explore 
how much risk banks pose today. Adrian and GS Head of the 
Financials Equity Group Richard Ramsden both see little 
cause for alarm, explaining that higher rates should boost 
banks’ net interest income and revenues given their asset-
sensitive balance sheets. Ramsden also doesn’t expect a 
significant deterioration in credit quality absent a broader 
economic downturn and says that most banks should have 
excess capital even after accounting for rate-driven portfolio 
losses.   
But even if banks can weather higher rates, what about 
markets more broadly? GS strategists and analysts are not 
especially concerned, with Chief US Equity Strategist Ben 
Snider arguing that current rate levels shouldn’t prevent 
continued strong US earnings growth and the S&P 500 from 
climbing higher, Chief Credit Strategist Amanda Lynam 
viewing the headwind to corporate credit from higher rates 
as manageable amid the solid growth backdrop, and US 
Financials Analyst Alexander Blostein expecting the recent 
slowdown in private market activity to rebound once rates 
stabilize. GS multi-asset strategists Christian Mueller-
Glissmann and Andrea Ferrario add that higher yields have 
strengthened the strategic case for bonds in a multi-asset 
portfolio, though strong earnings continue to favor equities 
for now. But they all warn that if rate volatility remains 
elevated, and especially if yields continue to rise from here, 
risk assets could face mounting pressures.  
All that said, with the AI boom powering the economy and 
markets, everything may come down to whether higher rates 
will derail it. Snider views this as unlikely given the strong 
balance sheets of the firms investing in AI and the expected 
profitability of these investments, with Lynam also seeing a 
high bar to derail AI-related debt issuance. Adrian also 
maintains a positive baseline for the AI economy but is 
concerned about the circularity of investment, increased 
leverage, and the sustainability of revenue growth in the AI 
complex. And Zandi again seems the most worried, warning 
that “AI could become the part of the economy that breaks” 
if rates remain at current levels for much longer.  
Allison Nathan, Editor  
Email: allison.nathan@gs.com     
Tel: 212-357-7504   
Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC

Higher bond yields: how concerning?

### Page 4

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 4 
Top of Mind Issue 152

Mark Zandi is Chief Economist of Moody’s Analytics. Below, he argues that the US economy 
is vulnerable rather than resilient and worries that high interest rates could deepen existing 
pockets of weakness and ultimately cause something to break in the financial system.   
The views stated herein are those of the interviewee and do not necessarily reflect those of Goldman Sachs.
Allison Nathan: Prior to the 
September 2026 Fed rate hike, you 
argued that a hike would do little to 
address supply-driven inflation but 
could damage already weak parts of 
the US economy. Where are you 
seeing the most softness? 
Mark Zandi: The clearest softness is 
in the labor market. Monthly payroll 
growth has averaged around 40,000 over the past year and has 
been essentially zero when excluding the healthcare sector. 
Hiring is moribund, hours worked are very low, and while the 
unemployment rate remains low, that is partly a reflection of 
declining labor force participation. Adjusting for that cyclical 
drop would put underlying unemployment in the mid-4% 
range—above the level consistent with full employment—and I 
expect the headline rate to move toward that level over the 
next 6-18 months. Real wage growth is, at best, flat, and for 
many Americans is actually declining, which is eroding 
purchasing power, particularly for lower- and middle-income 
households, who are increasingly drawing down savings to 
sustain spending. So, while the economy is still growing, the 
underlying labor market and household fundamentals point to 
very tenuous growth. 
 While the economy is still growing, the 
underlying labor market and household 
fundamentals point to very tenuous growth.”   
Allison Nathan: What is causing this softness? 
Mark Zandi: Policy choices are at the root of the weakness. 
Tariffs, while more consequential last year, continue to weigh 
on growth and boost inflation. Heavy-handed immigration policy 
is also an underappreciated source of weakness. Many firms 
losing immigrant workers cannot afford to raise wages enough 
to attract native-born workers, so they are raising prices to align 
demand with the supply they can produce with a reduced labor 
force. But the most immediate and significant driver of the 
softness is the Iran war, which has pushed up energy prices, 
further fueling inflation and weighing on growth.  
Allison Nathan: But US real GDP growth remains near 2%—
roughly the economy’s potential rate. Isn’t that a relatively 
healthy growth pace? 
Mark Zandi: Real GDP growth is indeed running at 2% yoy, but 
I estimate that AI-related activity accounts for roughly one-third 
of that, or around 0.6–0.7pp. Roughly half of that comes from AI 
capital spending, net of electronics imports for data centers, 
while the balance reflects wealth effects from the surge in AI-
related stock prices that have contributed to an almost doubling 
in the S&P 500 since ChatGPT debuted four years ago, boosting 
net worth and consumer spending, especially among high-
income households. So, the non-AI economy is growing at only 
around 1%–1.5%. That’s a pretty punk economy, and a difficult 
place to be. 
Allison Nathan: The September SEP indicated that most 
FOMC members expect one more rate hike this year, and 
the market is expecting the same. How damaging could 
further tightening be? 
Mark Zandi: While I expect another hike this year, with the 
economy already barely growing at potential, additional hikes 
could push growth below potential. That would further strain 
the already weak non-AI economy and trigger layoffs, 
significantly raising the likelihood of a self-reinforcing negative 
spiral. So, further tightening would be a risky endeavor. 
 With the economy already barely 
growing at potential, additional hikes could 
push growth below potential. That would 
further strain the already weak non-AI 
economy and trigger layoffs, significantly 
raising the likelihood of a self-reinforcing 
negative spiral.”   
Higher rates are already inflicting damage on the economy’s 
most rate-sensitive sectors, particularly housing and 
commercial real estate. Refinancing is in a deep freeze. Existing 
home sales have fallen to levels last seen during the teeth of 
the housing crash and the Global Financial Crisis. Home prices 
are moving sideways on average and outright declining in half 
the country. That could easily create broader problems for 
households, especially for those that bought homes in the last 
years after rates began to rise and have virtually no home 
equity. And while commercial real estate had begun to show 
signs of life, renewed rate increases are once again freezing 
activity and putting pressure on property values.  
Higher rates are also squeezing existing debtors as borrowing 
costs on credit cards, home loans, auto loans, and corporate 
debt rise. I am particularly worried about highly leveraged, often 
private equity-backed companies that have managed the higher 
rate environment by extending maturities and limiting near-term 
interest payments. That strategy cannot continue indefinitely if 
borrowing costs remain elevated or continue to rise.  
The equity market is another key area of concern. AI continues 
to propel the equity market, but it is not impervious to higher 
rates. Persistently elevated rates will eventually weigh on 
valuations, increasing the risk of a major correction that could 
quickly reverberate through the real economy as positive wealth 
effects reverse and consumer spending weakens. AI capex is 
also vulnerable as a higher cost of capital makes investment 
Interview with Mark Zandi

### Page 5

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 5 
Top of Mind Issue 152

more expensive and falling valuations make it harder to raise 
the capital necessary to sustain the current pace of the AI 
buildout. For now, AI is operating under its own momentum, 
supported by considerable optimism and euphoria. But if rates 
remain at these levels or higher for longer than a quarter or two, 
AI could become the part of the economy that breaks. 
Last but not least, higher rates will worsen the US fiscal 
situation, which is itself contributing to elevated yields today. 
While we have navigated this fiscal risk for some time, the 
fiscal situation is as bad as I have ever seen it. The deficit 
exceeds 6% of GDP, the primary deficit is above 3%, and debt 
is roughly 100% of GDP and on track to reach 125% in a 
decade. Annual federal interest payments are already well 
above $1tn and will rise rapidly if rates remain elevated. All told, 
I see a roughly one-in-five chance of a fiscal cliff event over the 
coming year—unlikely, but large enough to warrant attention. 
So, higher rates will weigh on the economy through several 
channels. Historically, rate increases of this magnitude have 
almost invariably caused something to break in the financial 
system. We cannot know exactly where the fault line will 
emerge, but the risk of these pockets of weakness becoming a 
broader problem is significant. 
 I view the distribution of possible 
outcomes as very flat and heavily skewed 
toward darker tail scenarios.”  
Allison Nathan: How do these risks translate into your 
forecasts for economic growth? 
Mark Zandi: My baseline forecast is relatively sanguine based 
on the assumption that the Iran war begins to wind down, 
allowing oil prices to decline to around $80/bbl as we move into 
next year. That should ease inflation, reduce Fed tightening 
concerns, and bring the 10-year Treasury yield back toward 
4.5% by early next year—a level the economy can digest.  
Importantly, though, I view the distribution of possible 
outcomes as very flat and heavily skewed toward darker tail 
scenarios. The war may not fade into the background, keeping 
energy prices elevated and raising the risk of further inflation 
pressure. That could prompt additional Fed tightening, push 
yields even higher, and ultimately cause something to break in 
the financial system.  
Meanwhile, the Treasury market has grown more fragile as 
traditional, price-insensitive buyers—including the Fed, banks, 
and foreign institutional investors—have stepped back, 
increasing the market’s reliance on highly leveraged, price-
sensitive hedge funds that could rush for the exits under stress. 
A debt-limit confrontation under a likely divided US government 
next year could provide the catalyst, particularly if lawmakers 
underestimate the consequences of a breach. In that scenario, 
an even sharper bond market selloff than we are currently 
experiencing could occur. While none of this is my baseline—
and I remain inherently optimistic because avoiding the darkest 
scenarios is largely a policy choice—I put the odds of a severe 
bond market selloff at around one-in-four and of the economy 
going completely off the rails for any of the reasons we have 
discussed at around one-in-three. So, the downside risks are 
significant. 
 I put the odds of a severe bond market 
selloff at around one-in-four and of the 
economy going completely off the rails for 
any of the reasons we have discussed at 
around one-in-three. So, the downside risks 
are significant.”  
Allison Nathan: The Fed has considerable room to cut 
rates. Should that provide any comfort that the worst-case 
scenarios may be avoided? 
Mark Zandi: That remains an open question. If inflation fades 
and allows the Fed to take its foot off the brake, rate cuts 
should bring yields down across the curve—though not back to 
pre-war levels. But in a darker scenario involving a debt limit 
crisis and even higher yields, short-term rate cuts would have 
little effect on the long end. The Fed would need to turn to 
alternative policy tools, including quantitative easing and bond 
purchases. That would amount to financial repression—a 
strategy similar to the one employed by the BoJ for many 
years—which could prove counterproductive by reducing the 
pressure on lawmakers to address the underlying problems 
while fueling even more persistent inflation. 
Allison Nathan: Why do you think others are more 
sanguine about the risks that higher interest rates pose to 
the US economy? 
Mark Zandi: Some likely see greater underlying economic 
strength and expect AI to drive much stronger growth. Others 
may believe that the neutral rate is higher, perhaps for the 
same reasons, while some may be more concerned about 
persistently higher inflation.  
But the more fundamental difference is the broader lens 
through which I currently view the economy. Many continue to 
describe the US economy as resilient, which I find fascinating, 
bordering on frustrating. In my view, the right word is 
vulnerable. Again, growth is pedestrian, the labor market is soft, 
which was further underscored by the much weaker-than- 
expected September jobs report, real wage growth is 
decelerating, inflation remains elevated, and interest rates are 
rising. That shift in perspective completely changes how one 
evaluates the risks ahead.

### Page 6

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 6 
Top of Mind Issue 152

Tobias Adrian is Special Advisor to the Managing Director and former Financial Counsellor and 
Director of the Monetary and Capital Markets Department at the IMF. Below, he argues that 
the global economy has been resilient despite higher bond yields and could very well remain 
so. But he sees several areas of concern in the economy and markets.  
The views stated herein are those of the interviewee and do not necessarily reflect those of Goldman Sachs, the IMF, IMF 
Management, or the IMF Executive Board.
Jenny Grimberg: The IMF has 
cautioned that sharp increases in 
global bond yields could create 
strains across economies and 
markets. How would you assess the 
global economy’s response to the 
recent rise in yields?  
Tobias Adrian: The global economy 
has so far responded to the rise in 
yields much the same way as it has to the shocks of the past 
seven years: resiliently. If somebody had told you in late 2019 
that the global economy would face a pandemic, multiple wars, 
deep geopolitical fragmentation, and trade and energy supply 
shocks, you would have been hard-pressed not to expect a 
global recession. The pandemic did, of course, trigger a 
contraction, but it was exceptionally short-lived despite the 
severe supply and demand shocks. And the global monetary 
tightening cycle that began in 2021 and accelerated sharply in 
2022 in response to the pandemic-related inflation surge did not 
trigger a recession at all, which is unusual by historical 
standards. So, the global economy has proven deeply resilient.   
Jenny Grimberg: What has that resilience owed to? 
Tobias Adrian: Three factors explain this resilience: policy, the 
adaptability of the economy, and some luck related to 
technological innovation. Over the past seven years, fiscal and 
monetary policymakers acted aggressively to stabilize economic 
activity. Fiscal policy provided substantial support during the 
pandemic, while central banks cut interest rates, expanded 
asset purchases, and established emergency standing facilities. 
That fiscal support has of course come at a significant long-term 
cost as debt-to-GDP ratios in advanced economies have surged 
since 2019, and deficits in many countries remain large, 
contributing to the rise in long-term yields. But policy has 
undoubtedly been critical to the economy’s resilience to date.  
The economy itself has also become more adaptable. A 
dividend of the pandemic is that firms have developed a greater 
ability to rewire global value chains quickly in response to a 
range of disruptions, from shifts in energy and food prices to 
geopolitical developments, which has helped the global 
economy more effectively absorb shocks.  
And luck has played a role in that the more recent challenges 
have coincided with the emergence and rapid adoption of 
generative AI technology, which has helped fuel expectations of 
a broad productivity boom and created massive demand for 
investment that is stimulating aggregate demand. If large 
language models (LLMs) had not come to the fore in November 
2022, global growth in the more recent period certainly would 
not have been as strong.  
Jenny Grimberg: As bond yields continue to climb, are you 
concerned that this resilience could falter? 
Tobias Adrian: So far, the rise in bond yields has not triggered 
a substantial, broad-based tightening in financial conditions or, 
more importantly, disruptions to market functioning or forced 
deleveraging that could damage markets and the economy. But 
I see three main areas of concern: the AI and technology 
complex, sovereign bond markets, and emerging markets (EMs).  
Three vulnerabilities stand out in the AI and technology 
complex. First, the interconnectivity and circularity of 
investment among many AI firms raises the risk that one firm’s 
earnings hit could fairly quickly spread across the broader 
complex. Second, many AI firms have taken on significant 
leverage, and investor leverage has also grown through the use 
of leveraged ETFs and derivatives. Leverage upon leverage can 
be problematic when adverse shocks hit. Third, while relatively 
subdued valuations suggest that the market is not in a 1999 
state of indiscriminate price appreciation, with investors 
continuing to differentiate across firms and sectors, the 
strength of the complex is predicated on strong earnings and 
high revenue growth, and it is not clear how long that will last. 
While my baseline outlook is positive, greater competition from 
open-source models, for example, could eat into AI firms’ 
profitability and pressure the broader value chain.    
Sovereign debt markets are another key concern. As I 
mentioned, global public debt is approaching 100% of GDP, and 
debt levels in major advanced economies exceed 120% of 
GDP. Those are staggering figures from a historical perspective. 
The investor base for sovereign bonds has also increasingly 
shifted to more leveraged investors who are more sensitive to 
macro developments. A key indicator to watch is auction tails—
the difference between the yield at which a government sells 
bonds at auction and the yield the market expected beforehand. 
Looking at 30-year bond auctions across G4 sovereign bond 
markets, the tails have grown over time, suggesting increased 
market vulnerability to supply. 5-to-10-year forward term premia 
have risen to pre-Global Financial Crisis (GFC) levels. So, the 
quantitative easing period has now been fully priced out of the 
long end of the global yield curve. And I see no good reason for 
term premia to decline in the foreseeable future. If anything, 
both expected short rates and the term premium will probably 
remain somewhat elevated.  
Finally, while EM economies have shown tremendous 
resilience through the shocks of the past seven years owing to 
stronger policy frameworks, resilient FX reserve buffers, and 
the development of local-currency bond markets that have 
reduced currency mismatches, vulnerabilities remain. 
Particularly striking is the K-shaped pattern of capital flows. 
Capital has flowed into EM sovereign bonds, while equity 
Interview with Tobias Adrian

### Page 7

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 7 
Top of Mind Issue 152

capital has flowed out on net as domestic investors direct their 
savings into global—and, in particular, US—technology stocks. 
So, even though EM equities have rallied over the past two 
years, capital flows have moved in the other direction. That 
leaves these countries’ external positions more leveraged, with 
less equity and more debt on net.  
Jenny Grimberg: Higher yields played a key role in the 2023 
regional banking crisis. Are you worried about banks today? 
Tobias Adrian: Not particularly. Banks generally do well when 
interest rates are elevated, because financial intermediation is 
fundamentally about spreads. When rates rise, asset yields 
often reprice faster than deposit costs, supporting bank net 
revenues. That partly explained the banking system’s overall 
resilience in 2023, even as a few outlier banks ran into trouble. 
Significant capital and liquidity buffers broadly absorbed mark-
to-market losses on fixed-income securities, while higher 
spreads supported profitability.  
Jenny Grimberg: What about residential and commercial 
real estate, where stresses were already visible? 
Tobias Adrian: Real estate markets are faring differently across 
countries. London’s housing market, for example, has cooled 
meaningfully, while other major financial centers like New York 
and Hong Kong continue to see strong home price appreciation. 
Real estate valuations are sensitive to long-term yields, and the 
pace of price appreciation will probably slow if yields remain at 
elevated levels. But with economic activity remaining relatively 
strong, immediate severe distress in either commercial or 
residential real estate seems unlikely.   
Jenny Grimberg: When would you begin to worry that 
higher yields are creating a financial stability problem?  
Tobias Adrian: The speed and magnitude of the yield move 
matter enormously. So far, even though bond yields have risen 
sharply, market functioning has remained orderly and no forced 
deleveraging appears to be occurring, as we discussed. I would 
be more concerned if the move in yields accelerated or became 
nonlinear, which could set off a damaging deleveraging spiral in 
which investors are forced to reduce leverage, adding to selling 
pressures and intensifying market stress. The question then 
becomes: who will catch the falling knife?  
And leveraged investors may not be the only source of selling 
pressures. Before central banks launched massive asset 
purchase programs to stabilize markets in March 2020, open-
ended funds were the sellers, while some hedge funds actually 
played a stabilizing role. Sharp selling pressure can even come 
from plain-vanilla mutual funds or ETFs. That said, it is 
reassuring that the major banks are well capitalized today, 
because it would be particularly worrisome if stress in the non-
bank sector coincided with forced asset sales in the banking 
sector. We saw that dynamic during the GFC and the Long-
Term Capital Management crisis.  
Jenny Grimberg: What should investors watch for warning 
signs of systemic stress? 
Tobias Adrian: I would watch repo market and money market 
functioning, because the early warning signs of stress typically 
show up in short-term funding markets. I would also look at a 
range of market-based indicators, including cross-currency and 
domestic basis measures, swap spreads, and convenience 
yields. I would also watch for a broader tightening of financial 
conditions, which can indicate that pressure is spreading 
beyond a particular market. And I would keep a close eye on 
implied volatility as a market-based assessment of risk that 
feeds directly into investors’ risk-management models. When 
implied volatility rises sharply, investors may need to cut 
positions, which can reinforce the underlying market move.   
Jenny Grimberg: If vulnerabilities were to become more 
acute, how well prepared are policymakers to respond? 
Tobias Adrian: Central banks retain a powerful ability to inject 
liquidity through repo operations, outright asset purchases, and 
emergency standing facilities, and in recent years they have 
repeatedly demonstrated that they can act quickly and 
decisively when market functioning deteriorates. That is distinct 
from the broader stance of monetary policy. Ongoing 
inflationary pressures mean that monetary policy remains on a 
tightening bias in many countries. But central banks can hike 
rates and inject liquidity at the same time.  
Fiscal policymakers are more constrained. Governments may 
still be willing to respond as forcefully as they did during the 
pandemic, but after taking such aggressive fiscal steps, less 
room exists to go big on fiscal policy today. Markets may also 
react to such support more negatively than in 2020 given 
already-elevated debt levels and ongoing concerns about fiscal 
sustainability. If monetary and fiscal authorities act together, as 
they did in 2020, that would temporarily mitigate market stress, 
but it would not resolve the longer-term fiscal problem.  
Hopefully, the recent rise in bond yields will feed back into the 
politics of fiscal policy and prompt an adjustment. Bond market 
pressure has already triggered policy shifts in some countries. 
The question is how far yields must rise before adjustment 
occurs more broadly, and whether that adjustment can happen 
without uncomfortable volatility. But until it occurs, fiscal 
policymakers will remain relatively constrained.     
Jenny Grimberg: Where does all of that leave you on the 
outlook for the global economy and markets? 
Tobias Adrian: It is important to distinguish between the 
baseline and the downside. My baseline is fairly positive. The 
global economy has demonstrated considerable resilience in 
the face of multiple severe shocks, and I would not be 
surprised if it continues to shrug off higher yields, provided they 
do not keep rising sharply in a disorderly fashion. At the same 
time, I do worry about downside risks. Beyond the 
vulnerabilities we have discussed, cyber risk is an important 
concern. So far, cyber risks have not been macro-critical, but 
they certainly could become so. AI could strengthen cyber 
defenses, but it could also enhance offensive capabilities. And 
while sophisticated institutions may be resilient to cyberattacks, 
many firms may lack the capabilities and know-how to defend 
themselves effectively. So, plenty of room exists to worry 
about the downside, but the offset is policymaking, especially 
monetary policymaking, as we have discussed. All of that 
leaves me vigilant but cautiously optimistic.

William Marshall explains the rise in global 
bond yields and where they may be headed 
Q: What has driven the gradual rise in global bond yields 
over the past several months? 
A: The most striking feature of the rise in yields has been its 
breadth. Yields in all major economies have risen, without a 
clear market leader or single source of the bond selloff, which 
has occurred across yield curves, with both front-end and long-
end rates moving to fresh highs. Measures of inflation risk and 
real rates, as well as rate expectations and term premia, have 
played roles in the rise in nominal yields. In particular, estimates 
of term premium, which reflect the extra compensation 
investors demand for holding duration, have surged to the 
highest level in over a decade. And the move has not been 
isolated to sovereign bonds, occurring across all forms of 
duration. Until recently, the relative stability in interest rate 
volatility suggested that fundamentals, including stronger 
growth, higher inflation risks, hawkish policy, AI capex, and 
long-run fiscal risks, largely drove the rise in yields. 
The rise in bond yields has not been unique to any market this 
year 
Year-to-date change in 10-year sovereign bond yields, bp

Source: Goldman Sachs FICC and Equities, Goldman Sachs GIR.  
Both term premium and policy rate expectations have contributed 
US 10-year term premium vs. risk neutral expectations, bp

Source: Goldman Sachs GIR. 
Q: What role has AI capex and related issuance played?  
A: A swing of the investment/savings pendulum back toward 
investment creates a less supportive backdrop for bonds and 
requires higher yields to motivate investors to hold duration. 
The perception that investment spending (and the issuance to 
finance it) is relatively insensitive to higher yields is reinforcing 
that impact. While evidence that the absorption of duration 
supply is having a durable impact on longer-term yields is 
limited, the backdrop of price-insensitive issuance from both 
sovereigns and corporates is among the factors likely weighing 
on duration risk appetite. As long as investment and borrowing 
needs continue to grow, the skew of risks around where longer-
term yields sit relative to macro drivers is likely to remain to the 
upside, with fundamental catalysts rather than valuations alone 
likely needed to reinvigorate duration demand. 
Q: Beyond the steady march higher, bond yields have risen 
sharply in recent weeks. Are these moves fundamental or 
technical in nature? 
A: The recent sharper rise partly reflects hawkish shifts in policy 
pricing, reinforced by the Fed, ECB, and BoJ all hiking in 
response to inflation pressure. Fundamental risks and central 
bank signals have modestly boosted front-end volatility. The rise 
in volatility toward the end of September points to these 
hawkish risks beginning to tighten financial conditions, as well 
as to technical factors related to hedging risks directly related to 
higher yields that may have helped to push yield levels higher 
than fundamentals would suggest. 
Interest rate volatility has risen sharply recently

The longer-term yield rise has lately de-coupled from fundamentals

Rising rates: key questions answered

### Page 11

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 11 
Top of Mind Issue 152

Q: Can the recent bond market operations of some 
Treasuries influence the level of bond yields? 
A: Managing the reliance on longer-term issuance is a sensible 
response to the higher term premium environment. The 
combination of structural shifts in longer-term savings and 
higher yields is reducing the duration needs of liability-driven 
investors such as pension funds. However, while maturity 
adjustments that rebalance supply and demand across the yield 
curve can benefit long-end bonds at the margin, they are 
unlikely to meaningfully lower yields. Buybacks and increased 
front-end issuance do not change the deficit or the inflation 
trajectory (see pg. 24). Although these actions can potentially 
reduce interest costs on average, they risk raising interest cost 
volatility should front-end rates need to rise to combat higher 
inflation. 
Q: On net, where do you expect global bond yields to go 
from here over the near, medium, and long term, and how 
do your forecasts compare to consensus?  
A: We expect bond yields to fall across major markets, with 10y 
yields ending 2026 at 4.75% in the US, 3.25% in Germany, 5% 
in the UK, and 3% in Japan. Achieving this will require greater 
market confidence in a more benign underlying inflation trend. 
Our more dovish-than-priced outlook for policy rates across 
most markets argues for lower yields over the medium term, 
though for the US in particular we expect fiscal dynamics to 
exert steady upward pressure on longer-term yields over time. 
Q: How is the balance of risks to your forecasts skewed, 
and what could cause yields to deviate meaningfully from 
these expectations? 
A: Front-end rates are still tethered to inflation risks, especially 
from energy prices, and yields will remain skewed to the upside 
if energy volatility does not subside. This is especially true given 
that the growth data remain strong and the Fed recently 
delivered its first hike. Although we expect policy tightening to 
prove limited, visibility on the extent and duration of the hiking 
cycle is low and rates often struggle to price in the end of 
tightening in advance of the final hike. A credible reduction in 
inflation risk via a decline in energy volatility across natural gas, 
crude oil, and refined products, or progress in underlying 
inflation toward central bank targets would therefore likely be 
required to realize lower yields. Evidence of slowing AI 
investment or any revision of the economic potential of AI 
investment would also alleviate upward pressure on yields and 
may ultimately lead to much lower yields down the line, as 
would a return of cyclical concerns.  
Q: What areas of the rates complex look vulnerable if 
government bond yields remain high? 
A: Aside from the effects on the real economy (see pgs. 9 and  
12-13), the market is likely to focus on the credit implications of 
higher rates in both AI and non-AI-related sectors. In Europe, 
higher yields pose risks to sovereign credit spreads. While the 
growth backdrop remains supportive for now, and fiscal 
fundamentals in some countries such as Spain have improved, 
the pressure from higher German yields and ECB rate hikes will 
pressure markets like OATs (French bonds), which will price 
additional fiscal risks at higher refinancing rates. 
Q: How concerned should we be about a sovereign bond 
crisis in France and contagion risk across Europe? 
A: The relative rise in French versus German yields has been 
extreme by historical standards, particularly when benchmarked 
against other sovereign spreads. Domestic macro and fiscal 
fundamentals remain unfavorable for OATs, with ongoing 
budget negotiations and the presidential race increasing 
uncertainty. That said, French spreads have widened more than 
fundamental drivers would imply, suggesting that the latest leg 
of widening has been driven by uncertainty and risk premium 
rather than a clear shift in the outlook.  
Stabilization likely requires inflation relief, signs of successful 
budget negotiations, or sufficient financial conditions tightening 
that shifts the path for ECB policy, with risk that elevated 
premium remains stickier. For Europe more broadly, spillovers 
have increased (particularly to Italian spreads), though risks still 
remain idiosyncratic to France. Similar to the case for France 
specifically, it will likely take a fundamental anchor to reduce 
risks of greater spillovers. 
The rise in French OATs vs. German Bunds has been extreme by 
historical standards 
2y and 10y OAT-Bund spreads, bp

Source: Goldman Sachs FICC and Equities, Goldman Sachs GIR.  
Q: How can investors best navigate a world of sustained 
high bond yields? 
A: The return of higher yields isn’t necessarily bad news for 
investors, especially if it reflects higher nominal growth. A world 
of high term premium used to be a familiar one, and higher 
term premium is associated with positive excess returns. This 
should help to protect total returns for bond investors. But the 
competition with cash is likely to imply steeper curves than in 
past periods of high term premium/low volatility. The hedge 
value of bonds will therefore need to be recalibrated, but bonds 
should still offer strong protection against cyclical downturns.

Pierfrancesco Mei expects higher rates to 
only weigh modestly on US growth next year 
The recent sharp rise in long-term interest rates has fueled a 
material tightening in US financial conditions, raising the 
question of how much this will affect US growth. Higher rates 
tend to weigh most heavily on residential investment, but also 
dampen consumer spending and capex, which can contribute 
meaningfully to the overall growth drag since they account for 
relatively large shares of GDP (~65% and 15%, respectively, 
vs. 5% for residential investment). Under our baseline forecast 
that sees the Fed delivering one more rate hike in December 
followed by three cuts starting in 2H27 and 10-year Treasury 
yields declining to 4.4% by end-2027, we expect only a modest 
0.2pp drag on growth in 2027 from higher rates, though the 
drag could rise to 0.5pp if rates remain around current levels. 
After moving sideways for a few months, financial conditions are 
now tightening amid the move higher in rates 
Changes in GS US FCI since January 1, 2026, bp

Source: Goldman Sachs GIR. 
Residential investment hardest hit… 
The sharp rise in 30-year mortgage rates to above 7% is likely 
weighing on residential investment, and will likely continue to 
do so by further cooling housing demand, which we already 
expected to remain tepid amid affordability concerns, slowing 
income growth, and a persistent drag on household formation 
from reduced immigration. Consistent with this, survey-based 
measures of purchase intentions (as expressed in the 
Conference Board and Michigan surveys) have declined since 
early summer.  
Higher mortgage rates will also continue to weigh on housing 
turnover via the lock-in effect: because many borrowers took 
advantage of low rates in 2020 and 2021 to refinance, the cost 
of moving at current mortgage rates is significant. Indeed, with 
mortgage rates up more than 1pp since February, over 90% of 
borrowers now hold mortgages priced below market rates, 
leaving them with little incentive to move.  
Although we noted during the last housing cycle that housing 
scarcity tended to dampen the hit to residential investment 
from rising rates, and such scarcity has eased, vacancy rates 
remain low by historical standards. On net, accounting for the 
prevailing vacancy rate, assuming mortgage rates evolve in line

measuring the counterfactual against our rates strategists’ pre-

2pp from annualized residential investment growth at the peak 
in 4Q26 and 1pp on average in 2027, with the drag likely to be 
roughly twice as large if rates remain at current levels.

...but consumer spending will also take a hit... 
Higher rates weigh on consumer spending mainly by reducing 
demand for durable goods. Using data since the 1960s, we find 
that a 50bp rise in the Fed funds rate—the cumulative increase 
we expect by December—has historically lowered consumer 
spending by just under 0.5%, with the decline concentrated in 
durable goods that rely heavily on credit financing and, in 
particular, recreational goods and motor vehicles.  
That said, we think the impact could be smaller today as strong 
household balance sheets should help cushion the rise in 
interest expense and negative wealth effects should be limited 
given our equity strategists’ baseline expectation that equity 
prices will continue to edge higher through 2027.  
We expect the decline in durable goods spending to outweigh the 
boost to spending from higher net interest income, resulting in a 
~0.2pp decline in consumption growth through 2027

overall net lenders as they hold more interest-earning assets 
than interest-bearing debts), we estimate that higher rates will

However, the drag would increase substantially if rates remain 
at current levels and stifle goods demand for longer. 
...as will capex  
Higher rates also raise firms’ marginal cost of funding, weighing 
on investment. A growing concern among investors is that 
higher rates could derail the AI-driven capex boom that has 
boosted investment growth in recent quarters, especially as 
companies have increasingly relied on debt and equity issuance 
to fund this investment. However, hyperscalers’ capex—which 
accounts for over two-thirds of AI investment in the US—has 
so far proven largely resilient to higher rates and input costs, 
suggesting that these companies are unlikely to meaningfully 
pull back capex in the near term (see pgs. 18-19). Consistent 
with this, our portfolio strategists continue to expect a healthy 
pace of AI-related capex in 2027, and our credit strategists 
recently raised their forecasts for hyperscalers’ debt issuance.  
That said, non-AI companies will need to devote a larger share 
of revenue to interest expense as they refinance maturing debt 
at higher rates. In the corporate sector, around 15% of bonds 
are set to mature within the next two years—roughly in line 
with recent years but below the historical average—as the 
average maturity of corporate debt has roughly doubled over 
the last three decades. This should slow the passthrough from 
policy rate hikes to corporate borrowing costs, reducing the 
economy's near-term sensitivity to the funds rate. However, 
small businesses are more exposed since around half of their 
debt is variable-rate and reprices quickly.  
We estimate that higher refinancing costs for businesses will 
dampen capex growth by ~0.3pp/year over the next five years

Taken together, we expect higher interest expense to weigh 
modestly on capex growth—by around 0.3pp in 2027 and 
subsequent years—assuming that borrowing rates reset in line 
with our strategists’ forecasts for Treasury yields and credit 
spreads, though the impact would more than double if rates 
remain around current levels2.

A modest growth drag, but larger if rates remain at current 
levels...  
Until recently, higher equity prices, tighter credit spreads, and a 
weaker Dollar roughly offset the drag on financial conditions 
from higher rates, resulting in only a 0.1pp hit to GDP growth 
this year (Q4/Q4). Looking ahead, we expect the combined hit 
to residential investment, consumer spending, and capex from 
higher rates to subtract around 0.2pp from GDP growth in 2027 
(Q4/Q4), which would still leave the economy growing close to 
our 2.3% potential growth estimate. That said, if rates were to 
remain at current levels, we estimate the drag would more than 
double, rising to just over 0.5pp. 
We estimate that higher rates will subtract around 0.2pp from 
GDP growth in 2027, with the drag over twice as large if rates 
remain at current levels  
Impact of higher rates on QoQ annualized real GDP growth, GS baseline vs. 
current market rates, pp

Source: Goldman Sachs GIR. 
...and watch additional risks 
Beyond the impact on the most rate-sensitive areas of growth, 
we see two additional risks if the current level of rates persists. 
First, equity prices could come under pressure. The most rate-
sensitive segments of the equity market—unprofitable tech, 
utilities, REITs, and homebuilders—have already pulled back 
sharply from their highs, though the S&P 500 has been roughly 
flat since August. Our strategists think that current rate levels 
should not prevent the equity market from rising further (see 
pg. 16), but if equities remained roughly flat through 2027, we 
estimate that the missing boost to household net worth would 
lower consumer spending growth by just under 0.5pp.  
Second, the fiscal sustainability outlook could deteriorate. Large 
primary deficits remain the main driver of the US’ rising debt-to-
GDP ratio, but the average interest rate on outstanding debt 
has remained below nominal GDP growth in recent years, 
allowing the government to issue new debt without 
mechanically pushing the ratio higher. This would remain the 
case under our baseline rate forecast, but not  if current rate 
levels persist and maturing debts  are refinanced at a higher 
cost (see pg. 15). In this scenario, interest expense and debt as 
a share of GDP would rise to an unprecedented 6% and 132% 
by 2035, respectively, increasing the urgency of deficit 
reduction, which could create a further drag on growth.   
Pierfrancesco Mei, US Economist

A look at growing global fiscal risks

### Page 15

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 15 
Top of Mind Issue 152

David Mericle and Pierfrancesco Mei assess 
the US fiscal outlook amid higher rates  
While concerns about the US fiscal trajectory have grown 
steadily in recent years, driven by unusually large primary 
deficits and a debt-to-GDP ratio approaching post-WWII highs, 
the recent sharp rise in rates has intensified those concerns. 
Under our baseline forecast of nominal GDP growth close to 
4.5%, an average primary deficit of around 3% of GDP, and an 
average interest rate on new government debt of just over 
3.5%, we expect the debt-to-GDP ratio to rise from 100% today 
to 124% by 2035, and an even higher 132% if current rates 
persist—a problematic situation that is politically difficult to 
tackle, but too risky to ignore. 
High debt is problematic… 
A large and growing debt is problematic because it puts upward 
pressure on nominal net interest expense as a share of GDP, 
which captures the cash outflows required to service the debt 
and is likely how policymakers think about the debt servicing 
burden. Nominal interest expense is already near its 1990s peak 
and is set to surpass it substantially in the coming years, 
especially if rates remain at current levels or rise even further.  
A larger debt stock can also raise interest rates, which could 
further push up debt levels. The average interest rate on 
outstanding debt has been below nominal GDP growth in 
recent years, which has allowed the government to cover its 
interest expense by issuing new debt without contributing to an 
increase in debt-to-GDP (even though the large primary deficit 
has continued to push it higher). This would remain true under 
our baseline rates forecast, but would not if current interest 
rates persisted as maturing debt is refinanced. 
To be clear, we don’t think such a rise would likely trigger a 
classic debt crisis—where falling confidence in the 
government’s ability or willingness to repay its debt causes a 
spike in interest rates that leads to financial market instability 
and/or recession—given that the US issues debt in dollars, sets 
its own monetary policy, and has a flexible exchange rate. But 
failing to address the rise is still risky and could prove costly. If 
the debt servicing burden or the necessary deficit reduction 
become large and painful enough, there could be pressure on 
the Fed to keep rates too low, resulting in high inflation. An 
unfavorable debt situation could also limit policymakers’ ability 
and willingness to fight recessions with fiscal stimulus. 
Nominal interest expense as a share of GDP will likely rise beyond 
its 1990s peak over the coming years… 
Federal nominal interest expense by nominal interest rate on new issuance, % 
of GDP

Source: Goldman Sachs GIR. 
…and the debt-to-GDP ratio will likely reach 124% by 2035, and 
even higher if current rates persist

### Page 16

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 16 
Top of Mind Issue 152

Ben Snider argues that the current level of 
yields should not prevent the US equity 
market from rising further, though another 
sharp rise would likely pressure the rally 
The S&P 500 is on pace for its fourth consecutive year of 
double-digit returns despite long-term Treasury yields surging to 
their highest levels in decades. For equity investors, the natural 
question is how much of a threat higher yields pose to stock 
prices, and at what level they might cause recent equity market 
strength to reverse. Ultimately, we think that the current level 
of yields should not prevent the equity market from climbing 
higher, though a further sharp rise would pressure the rally. 
Higher rates weigh on valuations... 
Rising rates most directly weigh on equity valuations. The value 
of an equity can be viewed as a discounted stream of future 
cash flows, and higher yields increase the discount rate applied 
to those future earnings. We estimate that over 75% of the 
S&P 500’s current value reflects cash flows 10+ years in the 
future, the largest share in decades. In other words, the market 
is highly dependent on distant future profits, making valuations 
particularly sensitive to higher rates today. 
Reflecting this pressure, equity multiples have declined 
substantially as bond yields have risen. The S&P 500 forward 
P/E multiple has fallen from 23x a year ago to 19x today, a 
nearly 20% decline. Although several factors have contributed 
to this decline—including AI-related uncertainty and skepticism 
about the durability of recent earnings strength—higher rates 
have played an important role. 
However, while equity valuation multiples have fallen, 
valuations relative to bonds have remained surprisingly steady. 
The difference between the S&P 500 earnings yield (5%) and 
the real 10-year US Treasury yield (3%)—a simple proxy for the 
equity risk premium—currently stands at 230bp and has 
remained fairly stable over the past two years. This suggests 
that equity valuations have largely recalibrated to the rise in 
yields thus far. However, the steady recent relationship 
between equity valuations and interest rates also suggests that 
a further increase in yields from here would put additional 
pressure on stocks. 
...but earnings matter more... 
However, valuations are only part of the story. The continued 
appreciation of the equity market to new record highs this year 
despite rising rates and declining valuations is a testament to 
the predominance of earnings in driving stocks. S&P 500 
earnings have grown by over 30% during the past 12 months, 
more than offsetting the rates drag on valuations. The more 
critical question for equity investors is therefore whether rising 
rates will derail the market’s underlying earnings strength.  
We see three reasons for optimism. First, US corporate balance 
sheets generally appear healthy today. Most S&P 500 company 
debt carries fixed rates and long maturities, and interest 
expenses remain small relative to strong profits. The S&P 500 
interest coverage ratio exceeds 10x, close to the highest level 
on record, and interest coverage for the median S&P 500 stock 
ranks in the 70th percentile of the last 40 years.  
Second, we estimate that AI investment spending is driving 
roughly half of S&P 500 earnings growth this year, and we don’t 
expect the recent rise in interest rates to affect those spending 
plans. While debt is financing a growing share of AI spending, 
the largest companies driving this spending have particularly 
strong balance sheets and appear to be targeting returns well in 
excess of their current cost of capital.  
Third, although the Fed recently hiked its policy rate, there is 
little evidence of economic overheating that would prompt 
policymakers to tighten aggressively to slow growth. And with 
the rates market already pricing over three additional 25bp hikes 
in the coming year, the bar for policy to surprise in a hawkish 
direction is high.  
…and rate volatility matters too 
Interest rate volatility also matters for equities. Over the past 
few decades, stocks have usually been able to digest gradually 
rising rates but have struggled to digest sharp rate increases, 
typically declining alongside increases in Treasury yields greater 
than two standard deviations. This relationship has been 
consistent across investment horizons and parts of the yield 
curve. Today, a two standard deviation move in the 10-year 
yield is roughly 50bp in a month, a “speed limit” only recently 
exceeded as yields surged in late September. So, further 
increases in interest rate volatility warrant close watch. 
Some pockets of greater vulnerability 
While the broad US equity market looks reasonably resilient at 
current yield levels, some pockets of the market are more 
vulnerable. Smaller companies typically have weaker balance 
sheets and more floating-rate debt than large-cap companies, 
leaving small-cap stocks more sensitive to rising rates. Real 
estate and housing stock earnings are also particularly tied to 
the interest rate environment. Recent rotations within the 
equity market have reflected these sensitivities. Homebuilder 
stocks, for example, have lagged the S&P 500 by more than 
20pp since June, closely tracking moves in long-term yields. 
This uneven rate sensitivity has pushed correlations across US 
stocks to near record lows. While concerns about rising rates 
have weighed on many stocks, the strong balance sheets and 
earnings growth of many companies exposed to the AI boom 
have kept the aggregate S&P 500 index near its record high. As 
a result, US equity market breadth has fallen to its lowest level 
since the Dot-Com Boom.  
Not derailing equities, for now 
Looking ahead, easing rate pressures would likely broaden the 
equity market rally and propel indices to new highs. However, 
even if yields remain at current levels, stocks should continue 
to climb gradually higher, as long as the earnings outlook 
remains intact and rate volatility remains contained. 
Ben Snider, Chief US Equity Strategist    
Email: ben.snider@gs.com Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC 
Tel:  212-357-1744

Amanda Lynam sees growing risk that interest 
rates shift from a tailwind to a headwind for 
credit markets but views the headwind as 
manageable as long as growth holds up 
For much of the past few years, elevated Treasury yields, or 
risk-free rates, have been a tailwind to corporate credit, 
sparking “yield-based” demand from institutional investors 
such as pensions, insurers, and foreign entities. The ability of 
these investors to achieve their all-in yield targets—even 
though much of the “heavy lifting” has been driven by the risk-
free rate rather than credit spreads—supported demand for 
corporate credit and helped keep spreads anchored in a tight 
range. Credit spreads account for less than 15% of the all-in 
yield of the USD Investment Grade (IG) Corporate Index today, 
compared to 45% from 2019 to 2021, on average. This has 
effectively turned segments of the corporate credit market into 
a quasi-rates product. But with interest rate volatility now 
increasing, we are monitoring the risk that interest rates shift 
from a tailwind to a headwind for credit in two ways: by 
reducing investor demand and weighing on corporate 
fundamentals. 
Credit spreads now account for less than 15% of the all-in yield of 
the USD IG Corporate Index 
All-in yield for the Bloomberg USD IG Corporate Index, deconstructed 
between the spread and risk-free rate components, %

Note: Data as of September 28, 2026. 
Source: Bloomberg, Goldman Sachs GIR. 
Rate volatility poses a risk… 
Increased rate volatility could reduce the confidence of yield-
based investors to deploy capital. This would be especially 
negative for the IG market, as a large part of the yield-based 
demand was targeted toward that universe (due to insurers’ 
rating-sensitive capital rules, for example). This flow is sizable, 
yet difficult to track. While weekly mutual fund and ETF flows 
are a closely watched, high-frequency data point for gauging 
credit market sentiment, they often do not capture flows from 
large-scale institutional allocators such as pensions, insurers, 
and sovereign wealth funds. The key signpost to watch for 
waning credit demand, in our view, is if rate volatility revisits 
the elevated levels of 2022-2023.  
Higher rates could also weigh on corporate fundamentals 
through a higher cost of debt capital and/or pressure on 
interest-sensitive business models which rely heavily on 
financing, such as those tied to housing and autos. These 
fundamental concerns are most pronounced for leveraged 
finance borrowers, who tend to have thinner financial cushions 
relative to their larger and more diversified peers. These 
borrowers also tend to rely more heavily on floating rate debt, 
which experiences a headwind from higher base rates 
immediately. Fixed rate borrowers, by contrast, experience 
higher interest costs only if/when they refinance their lower-
coupon debt. 
We also believe the fundamental improvements visible over the 
past several quarters are likely to stall somewhat for issuers in 
the broadly syndicated leveraged loan and private credit 
markets. Higher base rates are an especially unwelcome 
development for issuers aiming to refinance upcoming 
Software loan maturities—especially those formed during the 
low interest rate backdrop of 2020-2021, before the potential 
for AI-related disruption to legacy Software business models 
became a key concern. 
…though floating rate credit could benefit... 
While higher rate volatility could weigh on credit demand, 
modestly higher base rates may actually support increased 
demand for floating rate credit, such as direct lending loans 
(within private credit) or broadly syndicated loans (either 
outright or as part of structured investments), on the margin.  
The experience of the past few years in the broadly syndicated 
loan market shows how the linkage between higher base rates 
and demand can be nuanced. Notably, loan funds reported 
outflows when rates rose during the Federal Reserve’s 2022-
2023 hiking cycle, as recession concerns overshadowed higher 
loan yields. Given the magnitude and speed of the rate hiking 
cycle during 2022-2023, there was also a more pronounced 
focus on debt service and default risks.  
Incrementally higher rates in the current market backdrop are 
unlikely to generate the same concerns, in our view. Indeed, 
inflows into loan funds have accelerated since April 2026 and 
the momentum has extended into late September. Similarly, 
private credit fundraising reached a new record in 2Q26, but we 
primarily attribute this to the broad-based improvement in 
sentiment toward the asset class. 
...and growth is key 
The drivers of any rate increases are also far more important 
than the absolute levels of rates. Higher rates reflecting resilient 
growth are more easily digested by credit fundamentals. Given 
the solid state of the economy and the relatively contained 
amount of rate hikes that our economists expect, we view the 
fundamental headwind as largely manageable and unlikely to 
spark a meaningful rise in defaults above our current forecasts. 
A high bar to derail AI-related debt issuance  
All that said, given the sharp increase in yields across the curve, 
market participants are increasingly questioning whether a 
higher cost of debt capital could impact higher-rated issuers—
potentially derailing the pace of AI-related investment and, by 
extension, the AI-related debt issuance that we expect over the

next few years. We view the bar as very high for such a 
scenario, for two key reasons.  
First and foremost, we do not view today’s all-in cost of debt as 
outsized by historical standards. For example, while the 10-year 
US Treasury yield (5.30%) is the highest since 2007, the USD 
IG index-level spread of 83bp still ranks toward the tight end of 
the historical range since 2000 (and below the average of 
144bp). A similar takeaway is visible when isolating the spread 
of the USD IG Technology index. While the average spread of 
the sector has widened more than 20bp so far in 2026, the 
index-level spread measure of 91bp is below the average of 
128bp since 2000. Moreover, while longer-term ROIC metrics 
on AI investment are still widely debated, even a relatively 
conservative assumption of a 15% ROIC leaves plenty of room 
for the after-tax cost of debt to increase, while still likely 
resulting in accretive debt-financed investment opportunities.  
Second, highly rated Technology issuers have plenty of financial 
flexibility and debt capacity. For example, several AI-related 
firms—including MSFT, META, AMZN, GOOGL, NVDA, and 
TAISEM—have credit ratings in the range of AAA or AA. Such a 
high rating has become a relative rarity in the credit market, 
with only 11% of the market in that territory. Far more common 
these days is a rating in the A or BBB context—which allows for 
additional balance sheet leverage while still offering ample, 
consistent access to the USD IG debt capital markets. 
Only 11% of the market value of the USD IG Corporate Index is 
rated AAA or AA

The significant financial flexibility of the combined AAA/AA 
universe of issuers is visible when comparing key credit metrics 
side-by-side. The interest coverage ratio for the AAA/AA cohort 
is a striking 27.7x. This compares to 7.5x for the A-rated 
universe. Net leverage tells a similar story: 0.5x for the 
AAA/AAs, compared to 2.5x for As.

As Tech-related issuance has accelerated, the share of USD IG 
issuance rated AAA/AA has climbed to the highest since 2016—
but A and BBB rated issuers still dominate the market

We expect these highly rated, AI-focused firms to utilize the 
significant debt capacity available—irrespective of a higher cost 
of debt capital—as the risk of underinvestment in AI-related 
innovation appears greater than overinvestment.
